# Daily Papers
The project automatically fetches the latest papers from arXiv based on keywords.

The subheadings in the README file represent the search keywords.

Only the most recent articles for each keyword are retained, up to a maximum of 100 papers.

You can click the 'Watch' button to receive daily email notifications.

Last update: 2026-10-18

| **[MLExpert: Towards a Generalist Agentic Model for Autonomous Machine Learning Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22344v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are expanding from assisting scientific research and engineering to autonomously developing and improving AI systems, advancing AI for AI (AI4AI) as a pathway toward recursive self-improvement (RSI). Workflow-driven machine learning engineering (MLE) systems provide a practical path toward this pursuit through complex orchestration that manages long-horizon experimentation, while recent MLE systems adopt model-directed interaction, allowing models to autonomously organize experiments and adapt optimization strategies based on execution feedback. However, feedback from each experiment reflects several preceding decisions, and a long MLE run contains many such costly experiments. Training must therefore select useful supervision from long interaction trajectories and learn from intermediate outcomes. We propose a post-training method for autonomous long-horizon MLE optimization and apply it to train MLExpert, a 35B Mixture-of-Experts agentic model. We design the Kaggle-MLE Factory to construct executable tasks and an interaction harness for model-directed experimentation. Hierarchical agentic SFT transfers general tool-use and coding capabilities to MLE through outcome-guided supervision. Windowed Interaction RL (WIRL) treats the multi-turn decisions between successive experiment results as an execution window and assigns it a shared outcome-based reward. Asynchronous collection and policy correction support policy updates before the trajectory ends. On MLE-Bench Lite, MLExpert improves the average medal rate of the base model from 37.9% to 72.7%, achieving performance comparable to larger frontier models such as Kimi-K3 and GLM-5.3. Further experiments show that MLExpert improves performance on NatureBench Lite, agent scaffold optimization, and algorithm optimization, suggesting that the learned optimization capability generalizes to other tasks.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code:...</summary><p>Code: https://github.com/InternScience/MLExpert Model: https://huggingface.co/InternScience/MLExpert</p></details> |
| **[Co-Evolving Acting and Forecasting for Language Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20597v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Training language agents is bottlenecked by the high cost of long-horizon rollouts, yet standard agentic reinforcement learning (RL) learns only from a terminal scalar reward, leaving the supervision in intermediate steps largely unused. Prior work exploits this supervision through value targets that estimate per-step success probabilities or observation targets that fit environment response. Both are hard to construct well: (1) value targets come from the same single scalar signal for each step in the trajectory, and (2) observation targets, such as web pages and terminal logs, are long and noisy and reach only one step ahead. We propose CoPE, which adds a third kind of multi-step action targets. On successful on-policy trajectories, CoPE trains the policy backbone to generate the action sequences that follows each history. Each action is also supervised when forecast from the preceding histories, forming a temporal ensemble that the shared backbone absorbs during training, and this enables acting and forecasting to co-evolve. The policy still acts one step at a time at inference, with no overhead. Across five benchmarks (ALFWorld, ScienceWorld, WebShop, $τ^2$-bench, and AppWorld), CoPE (1) achieves stronger aggregate performance than GRPO and RL baselines with predictive auxiliary targets across simulated environments, while improving overall performance on both realistic applications; (2) delivers up to $1.9\times$ and $2.0\times$ higher sample efficiency than ECHO and GRPO, respectively, on ScienceWorld; and (3) achieves these gains with a single policy backbone, without additional models, separate pretraining, or inference overhead. The codebase is available at https://github.com/ulab-uiuc/CoPE.</p></details> |  |
| **[KnowGPO: Knowledge-Grouped Policy Optimization for Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22331v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Deep search agents solve knowledge-intensive tasks through long-horizon, multi-turn interactions, where assigning credit to individual search decisions is fundamentally difficult. Outcome-based reinforcement learning provides only a single terminal reward shared across an entire trajectory. Recent process-supervision methods aim to assign credit at finer granularity, but they typically anchor credit to either surface-level trajectory states or model-internal signals, both of which become unreliable in deep search where observations are heterogeneous and retrieved contexts are long and noisy. We propose KnowGPO, a reinforcement learning framework that aligns trajectories by the task-relevant knowledge they have acquired rather than by their surface observations. For each query, KnowGPO decomposes the information required to derive the answer into atomic knowledge conditions, and represents the state of a trajectory at each step by the subset of conditions it has satisfied. Trajectories sharing the same knowledge state are grouped for within-group comparison, and the incremental knowledge gain between consecutive steps serves as an environment-grounded process reward. Experiments on Xbench, BrowseComp-ZH, and GAIA show KnowGPO consistently outperforms outcome-only (GRPO) and process-supervision baselines (GiGPO, IGPO) by 3.8 to 4.2 points, with our 8B model rivaling or exceeding most 32B open-source systems.</p></details> | <details><summary>Code ...</summary><p>Code available at https://github.com/ZJU-REAL/KnowGPO</p></details> |
| **[Learning Self-Orchestration in Decentralized Agent Swarms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22322v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The capabilities of an agent swarm depend on both its individual members and how they coordinate. Yet existing multi-agent systems often train agents within fixed orchestration structures, separating the learning of individual capabilities from the organization of collective behavior. We introduce market-based post-training (MPT), a framework for jointly learning action policies and self-orchestration in decentralized agent swarms. Agents express their willingness to participate through bids, which determine who acts and how collective outcomes provide local training credit. Learning both action and bidding behavior allows individual capabilities and patterns of participation to adapt together without a central coordinating agent. We evaluate MPT on embodied crafting, robot navigation, and deep research. Results show gains over monolithic post-training and confirm that agent participation indeed evolves with learning. With the same post-trained action policies, learned self-orchestration outperforms alternative coordination strategies. Ablations show that both action and bidding benefit from learning, and that learned bids provide more effective action-training signals. Beyond the original training objectives, trained swarms adapt faster to related tasks and achieve better performance with larger populations at inference time. Larger populations also suffer smaller performance losses under agent dropout, demonstrating that learned self-orchestration supports reuse, scaling, and robustness in decentralized agent swarms. Code and trained models are available at https://github.com/Embodied-Minds-Lab/MPT.</p></details> |  |
| **[A team of Gemini 4 Argon agents beats Minecraft](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22319v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We placed four Gemini 4 Argon agents in a sandboxed Minecraft survival world and gave them one goal: defeat the Ender Dragon. Each agent controlled a player via an open-source library called Mineflayer and coordinated with its teammates through in-game chat and shared memory. No one assigned roles or wrote game logic. The agents planned, wrote, and debugged every program themselves. Researchers could watch the run on a live dashboard and pause or stop it at any time, but they had no cause to intervene. Starting from a randomly generated world on Easy difficulty, the team wrote 2,783 programs and defeated the dragon in 27 hours and 14 minutes, dying 605 times along the way. To our knowledge, this is the first end-to-end autonomous completion of Minecraft by AI agents, achieved using only the standard Mineflayer library without pre-scouted seeds or other game-specific tools written by humans. Winning required the agents to keep a coherent plan across thousands of dependent steps for more than a day. Complex real-world software engineering and enterprise tasks demand the same long-horizon coding and multi-agent coordination. We describe the setup, how the run unfolded, the team's strategies (dividing the work, sharing what they learned, recovering each other's items), and the limits of the result.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning to Steer LLM Agents and Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22316v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-based agents and multi-agent systems increasingly tackle complex tasks through long-horizon tool use and inter-agent collaboration. However, these systems are prone to deviating from the overall goal as execution unfolds, which wastes execution resources and leads to undesired outcomes, a failure termed goal drift. In this work, we propose a steering agent that supervises execution on the fly and steers the system back on track when drift occurs. The steering agent observes the execution context, deciding when to intervene with a low-cost fast path and how to intervene with a high-cost slow path. To unlock its steering intelligence, the ability to assess goal drift and generate effective interventions, we jointly train the two paths with reinforcement learning, combining a pairwise ranking objective with group-relative policy optimization (GRPO). Experiments on information-seeking search tasks show that the steering agent provides effective supervision for both single-agent and multi-agent systems, improving their performance and efficiency. The learned steering intelligence generalizes to out-of-domain tasks, transfers to unseen executors, and supports controllable intervention frequency without retraining.</p></details> | 21 pages, 6 figures |
| **[WorkflowPerturb: Calibrated Stress Tests for Evaluating Multi-Agent Workflow Metrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17990v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent LLM systems that generate structured workflows from natural-language requests are now deployed in production across cloud automation, DevOps, and enterprise orchestration. Operating them exposes a recurring change-management problem. Routine updates, such as re-running an input, swapping the LLM, or refactoring an agent's prompt or orchestration code, often produce workflows that differ substantially from validated references. Engineers then lack a principled way to decide whether a change is safe to ship. Automatic workflow evaluation is the natural tool, but in practice metric scores are poorly calibrated, and a numeric change rarely communicates the severity of the underlying degradation. We introduce WorkflowPerturb, a controlled benchmark that applies realistic, graded perturbations to golden workflows. It contains 4,973 golden workflows and 44,757 perturbed variants across three perturbation types (Missing Steps, Compressed Steps, Description Changes) at severities of 10%, 30%, and 50%. We benchmark multiple metric families, analyzing their sensitivity and calibration using expected score trajectories and instance-level alert rates. Our results characterize systematic differences across families and support severity-aware interpretation of workflow evaluation scores in change-management settings. WorkflowPerturb is publicly available at https://huggingface.co/datasets/microsoft/WorkflowPerturb .</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to EMNLP 2026 Industry Track. 18 pages</p></details> |
| **[Improving Environment-Agnostic Coding Agents: The Role of Scaffolding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22289v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Stronger language models may increasingly construct useful procedures for themselves, reducing the advantage of developer-supplied scaffolding. We investigate why this advantage persists and whether an environment-agnostic agent can be improved without environment-specific engineering. We evaluate a Codex-based agent in two nearly identical prompt variants on ARC-AGI-3, AGI Maze, and Terminal-Bench. Each variant is deployed with unchanged code, prompts, and general-purpose tools across environments, receiving environment-specific information as input. Changing only an instruction to encourage reporting difficulties and returning control for automatic continuation improves mean scores on both ARC-AGI-3 and AGI Maze, while Terminal-Bench shows small, mixed changes. Yet the general agent remains below an ARC-AGI-3-specific reference, including with GPT-5.6-sol. The reference prescribes organization and control rather than supplying new game rules or solutions. To investigate this gap, we transfer reset decisions to a scripted controller that follows a convention already given to the agent. This small change recovers part of the specialized agent's advantage. A complementary AGI Maze experiment shows that additional tutorial experience can reduce performance. Together, these findings highlight the distinction between having information and tools available and using them effectively. Supplied procedures still matter for the models tested here. The performance gains also come with higher token-based cost. The experiments do not separate better use of information and procedures from additional or differently allocated inference. We provide the EnvTraverse framework and an evaluated baseline agent for testing whether further environment-independent changes improve performance in some environments without degrading it in others.</p></details> | 37 pages, 5 figures |
| **[Learning Rational Strategies for Temporal Objectives in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22287v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>We study rational synthesis for stochastic multi-agent systems with unknown dynamics, where each agent is equipped with its own probabilistic temporal objective specified using Probabilistic Linear Temporal Logic on Finite Traces ($\mathsf{PLTL}_f}$). We impose solution concepts (e.g., Nash equilibrium, subgame-perfect equilibrium) as constraints on the joint strategy profile, using payoffs derived from each agent's probability of satisfying its temporal objective. In particular, we formulate per-agent rational synthesis as finding a strategy profile -- if one exists -- such that (i) the designated agent's probabilistic temporal constraint is satisfied, and (ii) the profile represents a stable solution under a chosen solution concept. We give conditional convergence guarantees for tabular Nash-Q learning, certify satisfaction with simultaneous finite-sample confidence bounds, and assess strategic stability with approximate best-response checking. Our approach operates in a stochastic \emph{Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning} (MARL) environment, where model-free learning algorithms attempt to attain extremal satisfaction probabilities for temporal objectives automatically compiled into reward automata. Our approach is empirically validated using 8 general-sum concurrent stochastic games.</p></details> |  |
| **[LEASH: Uncertainty-Aware Weak-to-Strong Escalation and Oversight for Computer-Use Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22280v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Computer-use agents increasingly tackle long-horizon tasks, where running a frontier model at every step is expensive and a small open-weight model alone often fails. Weak-to-strong escalation offers a middle ground where a weak agent acts by default and hands control to a strong one only when needed. The core challenge is deciding when to escalate, which requires an uncertainty signal cheap enough to check at every step. However, even after handoff, the strong agent can still get lost, but a black-box API that the strong agent is usually served exposes no internals to monitor. To this end, we propose LEASH, a training-free framework that unleashes the strong agent only when the weak agent's latent uncertainty signals it is needed, and keeps it on a leash afterward. LEASH has three layers: (1) initial task uncertainty for early routing, (2) step-level uncertainty for runtime escalation, and, after handoff, (3) a shadow observer for continued oversight of the strong black-box agent. Here, we use latent uncertainty as a fuel for all three layers. Specifically, we compare the weak model's hidden states with those from its past successful and failed trajectories, and a state that looks more like the failures signals higher uncertainty. During weak-agent execution, our uncertainty estimator reuses activations already produced during action generation, so it needs no extra forward pass or dedicated monitor training. Experiments on OSWorld across four contemporary model backbones show that LEASH consistently improves over standalone weak agents and outperforms random escalation by 6.55--11.94 points at matched escalation rates. With Qwen3.5-35B-A3B, LEASH achieves 73.66% task success at $0.4117 per task, matching always-on Claude Opus 5 (72.90%) while reducing inference cost by 19.2%.</p></details> |  |
| **[Auditable and Deployable Multi-Agent Motion Planning via Posetal Games from Legal Guidelines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22277v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>When robots are deployed in the real-world, they must interact with other agents and accomplish their own goals while adhering to legal constraints. These potentially conflicting objectives are often only partially prioritized, making scalarized objectives or fully specified hierarchies unsuitable. We present a framework for multi-agent motion planning under legal requirements based on games with partially ordered preferences (posetal games). Scenario-independent legal guidelines order abstract legal concerns, from which we automatically derive agent preferences and a legal game for any concrete scenario. Agents may refine their legal preferences with private objectives, and regulators may refine the guideline as laws evolve. We prove that such customizations can only remove equilibria and thus preserve legality. To coordinate agents without explicitly communicating private preferences, we introduce belief play, which provably recovers the true equilibria from conservative local games, and a distributed iterative best-response protocol requiring less information. We apply the framework to maritime navigation, systematically lifting pairwise traffic rules to multi-vessel encounters. On seven encounters with up to four vessels, every rule violation of the committed equilibria is certified as forced by the legal hierarchy or unsatisfiable, and defectors are identified from public information in all tested cases. These results indicate that guideline-based posetal games offer an auditable route from partially specified laws to multi-agent motion planning.</p></details> |  |
| **[Why Do Agent Teams Fail Across Boundaries? A Failure Taxonomy for Agent-to-Agent Systems with Private States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19342v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agents are increasingly interacting with other agents that act for different principals (people or organizations on whose behalf they work), such as a buyer's personal agent negotiating with an enterprise's seller agent. In such agent-to-agent (A2A) scenarios, each agent has its own environment and holds information and authority that the other cannot see. Moreover, the interests of each agent may be aligned (cooperative), partly aligned (mixed-motive), or opposed (competitive). Existing multi-agent system failure studies assume one orchestrator, shared workspace, or single principal. We ask how A2A systems fail when these assumptions are removed. We study more than 2,500 recorded runs of A2A systems built from different agent harnesses and models, spanning 488 scenarios across eight families (legal, CRM, security, commerce, etc.). From these traces, we derive $A^2$-Failures, a taxonomy of 20 A2A failure modes in four categories: (i) discovery and delegation, (ii) grounding and record consistency, (iii) verification, and (iv) conduct and fidelity. Three findings hold across every harness, model, and scenario studied: (1) discovery and interaction are driven by the phrasing of the task rather than the availability of peer agents; (2) no agent verifies a peer's factual claim on its own initiative, so an A2A system inherits the reliability of its least reliable participant unless the protocol or mechanism performs the check; and (3) the aggregate outcome score credits the agent completing the task while staying blind to the coordination, verification, and conduct failures beneath it, so A2A systems must be evaluated per agent, against each agent's own principal. We release the run logs behind the taxonomy at https://huggingface.co/datasets/a2a-salesforce/a2-failures.</p></details> | <details><summary>20 pa...</summary><p>20 pages, 7 figures, 4 tables. Run logs: https://huggingface.co/datasets/a2a-salesforce/a2-failures</p></details> |
| **[Agentic-MatriBO: Capability-Compiled Agentic Bayesian Optimization for Materials and Chemical Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.17832v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Materials and chemical optimization must choose the next synthesis, characterization, or high-fidelity simulation from sequential observations under a limited budget. Large language models can use task knowledge to propose adjustments, but composing surrogates, acquisition functions, and search strategies is constrained by compatibility, parameter dependencies, and evidence requirements. We present Agentic-MatriBO, a capability-compiled agentic Bayesian optimization (BO) framework that represents BO components and selection conditions as composable capabilities. A language policy proposes BO recipes and candidate plans; a compiler checks proposals against the registered capability catalog; and a deterministic execution layer validates and commits candidates. Oracle observations are stored in research memory and inform later rounds. We compare Agentic-MatriBO with eight baselines on four offline finite candidate pools---COF, PCE10, Buchwald--Hartwig (BH), and Suzuki--Miyaura---using five seeds per condition; results are reported descriptively. Across the four tasks, Agentic-MatriBO has the best mean endpoint quality after orienting each objective so that higher is better. On COF, it reaches the tabulated optimum in all five seeds both in the end-to-end comparison and after shared initialization, where it also retains an advantage in cumulative quality. Its normalized area under the best-so-far quality curve during the BO stage (nAUC-BO) is substantially higher on COF, near parity with the best baseline on PCE10 and BH, and lower on Suzuki. COF ablations show that removing the capability compiler or evidence gate coincides with lower cumulative quality and fewer exact-optimum hits. The framework provides an inspectable interface for evidence-conditioned BO adaptation.</p></details> | <details><summary>28 pa...</summary><p>28 pages, 9 figures, supplementary information included</p></details> |
| **[Librarian Agents: Training LLMs to Curate Long-Context Workspaces that Anticipate Future Queries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22273v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Reasoning over long document contexts remains a challenge for large language models (LLMs). Standard retrieval methods are inherently reactive, bottlenecking agents with complex, cross-document reasoning at inference time, while precomputed structures like knowledge graphs often fall short because they rely on rigid heuristics. Instead, can we allow agents to perform anticipatory directed curation: learning to proactively reorganize unstructured corpora prior to inference to efficiently serve diverse future queries. We build on a key asymmetry in modern agentic harnesses (e.g., Claude Code): while LLM agents are adept at navigating existing filesystems to solve an immediate task, they are rarely trained to construct reusable workspaces for future use. To instantiate this capability, we introduce Librarian Agents, a framework in which a builder agent transforms raw documents into a hierarchical filesystem of synthetic files and reference links, which forms a workspace tailored for a downstream answering agent. We formulate workspace construction as a Markov decision process and optimize the builder policy via reinforcement learning to directly maximize downstream answering accuracy. Empirically, Librarian Agents outperforms the strongest retrieval and precomputation baselines by 33 percentage points on BrowseComp-Plus, amortizes upfront construction costs across repeated queries, and generalizes zero-shot to unseen domains on BRIGHT with a 31-point average accuracy improvement.</p></details> |  |
| **[X-RIC: An Agentic RAN-Intelligent Controller Benchmark for Enhancing Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22269v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The O-RAN ALLIANCE introduces architectural flexibility through disaggregation and AI-driven control. While the RAN Intelligent Controller (RIC) enables centralized optimization, current AI approaches lack reasoning capabilities and explainability. We propose eXplainable RIC (X-RIC), a novel framework integrating agentic AI into O-RAN architectures. X-RIC combines reactive Reinforcement Learning (RL) with strategic agentic reasoning through: (i) automated Behavioral Cloning (BC) using domain heuristics for sample-efficient training, (ii) a Telecom Language Model (TLM), a transformer-based reasoning module providing contextual abstraction and interpretability, and (iii) closed-loop co-adaptation within the Near-RT RIC control loop enabling continuous policy refinement. To ensure deployment readiness, we introduce BARX (Benchmarking Agentic Reasoning for RAN), a domain-specific evaluation framework assessing agentic control across latency, compliance, and determinism metrics. We validate X-RIC on SLA-aware network slicing in simulation and O-RAN-compliant testbed deployments. X-RIC achieves 89% operational cost reduction while maintaining SLA compliance, converging 75% faster with 50% fewer training steps and 46% lower variance.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 10 figures. Accepted for publication in the proceedings of the IEEE International Conference on Mobile Ad-Hoc and Smart Systems (MASS), October 21-23, 2026, Hong Kong SAR, China</p></details> |
| **[Language Is a Security Surface for Agent Guards](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22244v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Step-level guards decide, before each tool call, whether an agent's next action is safe given the user's request and the trajectory so far. Users write those requests in many languages, while guard prompts and the benchmarks on which purpose-built guards are trained and evaluated are English. We ask whether a guard's verdict depends on the language of the request. We build LinguaGuardBench from the 1,220 labelled steps of TS-Bench AgentDojo-Traj: we translate only the user instruction into German, French, Turkish, Swahili and Amharic, and keep every tool call, observation and label byte-identical, so any change in a verdict is attributable to language alone. Six English rewordings and a whitespace placebo serve as controls, and managed guards are run three times to measure their noise. Across 19 guards, from frontier APIs to 4B open models and four purpose-built agent guards, most open, small and purpose-built guards change verdicts under translation beyond their own noise floor, and the effect grows with the rarity of the language: among the nine guards below 13B parameters, the mean placebo-adjusted flip rate rises from +1.5 pp on French to +4.4 pp on Amharic. Purpose-built guards are the most sensitive. The direction of the damage tends to follow guard size: open guards of 12-30B parameters block more benign steps (up to +5.0 pp false positives), and every significant loss of recall is in a guard below 10B (up to -7.4 pp). Frontier guards shift by at most about one point; the strongest, Gemini 3.8 Flash, returns identical verdicts with and without the request. The damage is concentrated in a few instructions and, in a post-hoc analysis, falls on steps where the agent's reasoning restates the English request, consistent with guards reacting to inconsistency between channels. English paraphrase moves every guard less than translation does. We release the benchmark and all 489k verdicts.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 Workshop on Agents in the Wild: Safety, Security, and Beyond (AIWILD). 9 pages plus appendices</p></details> |
| **[Shortcut Sensitivity in Agent-Safety Evaluations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22243v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI agents take actions through tools. Some actions are sensitive, so guards that supervise them must themselves be evaluated reliably. Yet the data used to evaluate a guard can include cues that make labels predictable without measuring the intended safety construct, and those cues can also influence guard decisions. We introduce a three-level shortcut-auditing framework and audit completed LinuxArena trajectories and two TS-Bench-derived step datasets. We distinguish label predictability, observational guard association, and paired decision sensitivity. In LinuxArena, shorter average shell commands strongly predict trajectory labels. In AgentDojo-Traj, exact-tool lookup falls from F_1=.901 with mixed-domain folds to .495 under domain holdout. In AgentHarm-Traj, released TS-Guard F_1 falls from .902 under row weighting to .719 with equal interaction weighting, while balanced accuracy rises. We then test the strongest safe-row marker association in a seven-configuration screen on a frozen 64-step AgentDojo-Traj cohort using repeated, interleaved Haiku calls. Rewriting the <INFORMATION> marker lowers blocking by 8.33 percentage points, while opaque tool aliases raise it by 11.46 points. All unsafe calls remain blocked; the changes concern false positives against inherited labels. Both effects remain nonzero after subtracting the formatting-placebo effect, with exploratory 95% ranges excluding zero. The same edits also change measured accuracy against inherited labels. This provides direct evidence that benchmark representation can materially change a guard's decisions and measured evaluation performance. The Haiku-sized effects do not reproduce in Luna or deterministic Q8 TS-Guard. We recommend domain group holdouts, evaluation-unit metrics, and placebo-controlled paired interventions.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted at the NeurIPS 2026 workshops TAE (Can We Trust AI Evaluation?) and AIWILD (Agents in the Wild: Safety, Security, and Beyond)</p></details> |
| **[MMSearch-OPD: Easy Made Hard, Hard Made Learnable for Long-Horizon Multimodal Search Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22240v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multimodal deep search is moving from short queries to long-horizon investigations that continually acquire evidence. However, training such agents remains constrained by limited challenging training questions and insufficient process feedback. We introduce MMSearch-OPD, combining controllable data refinement with joint reinforcement learning (RL) and on-policy distillation (OPD). Our Refine pipeline performs targeted expansion of reasoning chains derived from existing simple multimodal questions. Parameterized transformations control search depth and cross-modal evidence requirements while reusing existing image--question pairs. For training, answer rewards and lightweight behavior penalties guide RL, while the reward gap modulates token-level teacher guidance on imperfect student trajectories. An exponentially decaying distillation coefficient reduces teacher dependence as training proceeds. Experiments show gains from data refinement and further improvements from joint training after SFT, indicating stronger long-horizon multimodal deep search capabilities.</p></details> |  |
| **[OS-Helix: Unifying GUI Interaction and Code Execution in Native Computer-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22234v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Real desktop automation is neither purely visual nor purely programmatic. Agents must understand user-facing interfaces while efficiently manipulating files, scripts, and system state. However, native GUI/code coordination cannot be obtained by prompt-level tool composition. In our preliminary experiments, naively adding a code tool degrades a GUI agent, revealing that GUI/code routing must be internalized through training on high-quality hybrid data. We present OS-Helix, an end-to-end trained multimodal computer-using agent that unifies GUI actions and code execution in one action space. To construct the required supervision, we introduce application-artifact composition, pairing desktop applications with authentic file artifacts to instantiate executable Ubuntu states, and a decoupled instruction/evaluator generation pipeline that produces environment-grounded tasks whose final states can be verified regardless of the solution modality. We cold-start the policy with SFT on filtered hybrid data and further improve it through online RL in live desktop environments. For RL, we propose Step-balanced GSPO, which aggregates importance weight by action step rather than raw assistant tokens to prevent long code actions from dominating heterogeneous-action optimization. On OSWorld-Verified, OS-Helix-27B and OS-Helix-35B-A3B achieve relative improvements of 35.0% and 33.7% over their matched Qwen3.5 hybrid baselines, while OS-Helix-27B-RL further improves by 42.9% to reach 66.44% success. Ablations provide evidence consistent with contributions from learned GUI/code coordination, scalable verifiable supervision, and action step balanced optimization, while demonstrating cross-platform transfer on WindowsAgentArena. Our project homepage is at https://os-copilot.github.io/OS-Helix/.</p></details> | <details><summary>Proje...</summary><p>Project page: https://os-copilot.github.io/OS-Helix/</p></details> |
| **[Beyond Downstream QA: Rethinking How We Evaluate Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22209v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agent memory benchmarks evaluate whether an agent's responses reflect correct knowledge, measuring downstream question-answering accuracy as a proxy for memory quality. We show this evaluation paradigm is incomplete: it conflates memory correctness with retrieval and generation quality, producing both false positives where correct responses mask broken memory and false negatives where incorrect responses blame correct memory for retrieval failures. We introduce FEB-30, a benchmark of 30 fact-evolution trajectories spanning 6 diagnostic categories and 3 difficulty levels, constructed via paradigm inversion: documents are generated from known fact trajectories, yielding deterministic ground truth without human annotation. Each trajectory defines how a fact should evolve across multi-author artifacts (documents, emails, conversations), enabling direct inspection of the memory store after each ingestion step. | **[From Agent Traces to Trust: A Survey of Evidence Tracing and Execution Provenance in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.04990v6)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-based agents are moving beyond text generation to planning, tool use, retrieval, memory access, environmental interaction, and multi-agent collaboration. Because their outputs depend on information and execution steps across an agent run, their execution processes are difficult to verify, debug, and audit. Even a correct final answer does not reveal which evidence supported its claims, whether tool calls were justified, how memory shaped decisions, or where execution failures originated. Relevant research addresses these questions across evidence attribution, tool-use safety, agent memory, and observability, but these areas emphasize different parts of the execution process. This survey brings them into a common framework through execution provenance, a typed graph of an agent run, and evidence tracing, its projection onto evidence-support relations, distinguishing evidence that supports a conclusion from execution that influences it. We introduce a six-dimensional taxonomy covering trace sources, evidence and execution units, provenance relations, tracing granularity and timing, representation forms, and trust functions, complemented by operational definitions of nine provenance relations and two cross-cutting attributes, provenance origin and trace assurance. Applying this framework to the surveyed systems and benchmarks reveals three gaps: limited integration of fine-grained provenance with multiple trust functions, no benchmark in our catalog that evaluates the complete provenance chain, and little reporting of tamper-evident or attested traces. These findings motivate research on unified trace schemas, semantic provenance, memory and multi-agent lineage, provenance-aware safety and recovery, realistic benchmarks, trace assurance, and privacy-aware auditing. Resources are available at https://github.com/xiaoqi-7/Agent-Tracing-Survey.</p></details> |  |

Across these settings, we find that frontier models achieve the highest success rates, but their latency causes them to lose moving targets, while small open models often do not declare arrival even when they reach the target. Guided by this failure analysis, we fine-tune a 2B model on drone action data synthesized from public RGB-D images; its success rate rises from 0% to 31% in simulation and outperforms the frontier models in real flight. | **[AgentPerturb: Evaluating LLM Agent Robustness under Controlled Interaction Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21624v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Real-world tasks are often complex and noisy, posing substantial challenges for LLM agents. Project repository: https://github.com/xuyj233/AgentPerturb</p></details> |
| **[Evaluating Inquiry Learning in LLM-Based Agents: Hypothesizing, Reasoning, and Exploration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21604v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scientific inquiry involves iterative cycles of exploration, hypothesis generation, and reasoning over observations to uncover relationships in complex systems. We extend the Countdown task to a mathematical environment where an agent probes the system by manipulating variables, collecting observations, and inferring relationships between them. This setup enables analysis of exploration strategies, causal discovery, and hypothesis refinement during inquiry. Across seven LLMs and multiple prompting strategies, our experiments reveal limited inherent inquiry capabilities and low success rates in discovering the underlying expressions. | **[SecureClaw: Clawing Back Control of LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09549v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Tool-using large language model (LLM) agents face two distinct security failures: unauthorized external actions and exposure of sensitive plaintext inside the runtime before any final output check can intervene. We present SecureClaw, a dual-boundary architecture that enforces authorization in a trusted executor and keeps raw protected data outside the LLM runtime. Sensitive reads pass through a trusted gateway that replaces raw values with reference IDs. In the evaluated deployment, the gateway also provides short planning summaries that deliberately release selected information, a process called declassification. Writes that change external state follow a PREVIEW$\rightarrow$COMMIT protocol in which only a trusted executor may execute the exact request authorized by policy. The runtime can still plan over summaries and reference IDs, but cannot directly resolve the IDs into secrets or perform side effects. Across the AgentDojo, AgentLeak, and Agent Security Bench (ASB) benchmarks, SecureClaw is the only defense we evaluate in a common harness that simultaneously retains usable task utility and achieves 0% attack success rate (ASR) on ASB, 0.64% ASR on AgentDojo, and 3.23% overall leak on AgentLeak's attacked parity lane, which measures final-output and internal-relay leakage. This version corrects the conference footer only; scientific content is unchanged</p></details> |
| **[DialFork: Going Beyond Factuality to Evaluate Procedural Hallucination in Conversational Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21561v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) deployed as conversational agents are increasingly expected to follow procedural logic. We introduce DialFork, the first benchmark to measure this failure mode on raw dialogue text. DialFork is built by forking conformant dialogues at controlled turn positions and injecting one of four hallucination types, yielding 36,278 dialogues across 24 tasks in 12 domains; 30 human annotators validate the construction. We define three evaluation tasks that characterize procedural deviation at three granularities: how far a set of dialogues deviates from conformant ones (monitoring deployed agents), how far a single dialogue does (procedural retrieval), and at which turn a dialogue first goes off-track (real-time guardrails). For the first two, we propose dialog-flow-aware metrics, FlowKL and FlowScore, that compare dialogues by how they unfold rather than their content. At the dialogue level, FlowScore significantly outperforms all baselines across seven encoders, showing that semantic similarity is no substitute for procedural similarity. | **[Distributed scalable coupled policy algorithm for networked multi-agent reinforcement learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.05447v3)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>This paper studies networked multi-agent reinforcement learning (NMARL) with interdependent rewards and coupled policies. Moreover, each agent interacts exclusively with its direct neighbors to obtain accurate policy parameters, while maintaining local estimates of other agents' parameters to execute its local policy and collect samples for optimization. These estimates and policy parameters are updated via a push-sum protocol, enabling distributed coordination of policy updates across the network. | **[Agent Status Protocol (ASP): A Lightweight Specification for State Reporting in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21538v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Agent Status Protocol (ASP) defines a lightweight way for heterogeneous AI agents to report their operational state to a status server, and for observers such as dashboards to receive that state as soon as it changes. It reuses existing vocabularies: task states from the Agent2Agent (A2A) protocol, floor states from the Open Floor Protocol (OFP), and attribute conventions from the OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic conventions. KGW in the prompt survives GEPA's rewrites in 0 of 12 copies, AgentMark as released in 5 of 12. We introduce Jev-LiteMAS, a multi-agent framework that bridges both gaps by combining a lightweight decision model (Jev) with generative execution and feedback. To address the planning gap, Jev selects experts dynamically from the task and prior expert outputs, using accumulated context to ground subsequent execution without a pre-generated trajectory. To address the review gap, Jev screens intermediate artifacts against task criteria: passing artifacts proceed directly, whereas flagged ones undergo LLM re-review for acceptance or corrective feedback. Across four benchmarks and two backbones, Jev-LiteMAS reduces generative-LLM tokens by 12.7-54.6% over LAS, maintaining accuracy within 1.34 percentage points and lowering latency in seven of eight settings. | **[PriorityFEC: Deadline-Aware Streaming FEC for KV-Cache Handoff in Edge Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21390v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-agent large language model (LLM) systems are increasingly deployed at the edge to support complex tasks that require collaboration and information exchange among multiple agents. PriorityFEC first constructs a utility-aware source stream by placing high-utility KV blocks at earlier transmission positions. It then protects the ordered stream using a feedback-adaptive Streaming Forward Erasure Correction (FEC) mechanism, which schedules repair packets according to receiver feedback before the TTFT deadline. We implement PriorityFEC on a distributed edge LLM serving prototype and evaluate it with Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct across multiple datasets. Results show that PriorityFEC achieves superior task quality over the compared transport schemes, with its advantages becoming particularly pronounced under stringent deadline constraints. | **[Constructing and Evaluating Clinical Reasoning Trajectories for Medical Agent](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05090v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Evaluation of medical artificial intelligence agents remains predominantly answer-centric, assessing only the correctness of final outputs while overlooking the quality of intermediate reasoning. Experiments across CareQA, PubMedQA, and CECMed demonstrate that trajectory context consistently improves reasoning coherence, with gains of +0.029 to +0.041 over a zero-shot baseline. | **[OpCredit: Operation-Aware Policy Learning for Agentic Knowledge Graph Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21345v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Agentic knowledge graph question answering(KGQA) relies on sequences of executable operations, but trajectory-level rewards obscure their individual contributions. The framework implements bias control by presenting one style-normalized reasoning trace per distinct answer, and bias injection by placing each aggregating agent's initially selected answer in the minority. ## Drug Design
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[PMO-Dock: Benchmarking Docking, Specificity, and Generalization in Molecular Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.19011v1)** | 2026-10-13 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The Practical Molecular Optimization (PMO) benchmark standardized evaluation in molecular optimization, but it is built on simple property-based oracles that do not capture structure-based drug design. As the field has moved to docking-based objectives, evaluation practice has become unstandardized, and different methods report results on different docking tasks, often without a shared protocol for fair comparison or sample-efficiency constraints. To address this, we introduce Practical Molecular Optimization for Docking (PMO-Dock), a benchmark and protocol for docking-based optimization consisting of 25 tasks covering hit generation, lead optimization, and a new specificity task requiring strong on-target binding while penalizing off-target interactions. The protocol separates model development from final evaluation: validation tasks are used for hyperparameter tuning, and test tasks are held out for final evaluation and are not used during development. Evaluation on the test tasks is done under a strict oracle budget to reflect realistic drug-discovery settings. We benchmark four diverse high-performing methods spanning different optimization paradigms, Saturn (reinforcement learning), GenMol (discrete diffusion), Genetic-guided GFlowNet, and ChemLlama (LLM-based evolutionary search). Our results show no universally best method across tasks, additional analyses of hyperparameter transfer (cross-task and cross-category) and sensitivity show that transfer is often limited and varies by method, motivating method-dependent selection strategies. This benchmark provides a concrete standard for evaluating generalization to unseen tasks and sample efficiency under a limited test-time oracle budget.</p></details> |  |
| **[A Large Scale Investigation of Scaling Limits in Chemical Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.13408v3)** | 2026-10-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Chemical Language Models (CLMs) are increasingly used in de novo drug design, driven by recent growth in model scale, compute, and dataset size. However, the relationship between design choices, training dynamics, and downstream generation quality remains poorly understood. We present a compute-controlled scaling study of CLMs comprising more than 30,000 experiments across molecular representations (SMILES, SELFIES, SAFE), tokenizations (atom-level and byte-pair encoding), model scales (0.5M-1B parameters), leakage-controlled datasets (MOSES, ChEMBL, PubChem, ZINC-22), and architectures (decoder-only and encoder-decoder). By fitting IsoFLOP profiles, we establish clear scaling trends in pretraining loss, but find that these improvements do not translate into comparable gains in goal-directed molecular design. Layer-wise probing and sparse autoencoder analysis reveal continued development of chemical representations: chemical syntax saturates early, while semantic properties emerge more slowly and become increasingly accessible with further training and model scale. These representational gains coexist with diminishing improvements in goal-directed generation under the evaluated protocols and oracle budgets. Our resulting suite of models, NovoMolGen, achieves state-of-the-art results, outperforming prior CLMs and specialized generative models in goal-directed molecular generation across drug discovery tasks. These findings expose a disconnect between chemical representation learning and downstream molecular design, motivating the development of pretraining paradigms that more directly learn chemical semantics.</p></details> |  |
| **[One Sequence, Many Decodings: CAGenMol-2 Recasts Drug Design as Masked Molecular Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34301v1)** | 2026-09-28 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Drug design couples property evaluation, conditional generation, structure-based design, and local optimization, yet machine learning systems typically address these capabilities with separate task-specific models. We introduce CAGenMol-2, a masked diffusion molecular language model that represents molecules, continuous scalar properties, and 3D protein pockets within a single wrapped sequence. Within this pretrained interface, downstream operations are selected by which sequence regions are observed or masked at inference, allowing one checkpoint to perform property prediction, property- and pocket-conditioned generation, and partial-constraint design without task-specific architectures or backbone fine-tuning. We further propose Adaptive Fragment Optimization (AdaFO), a gradient-free mask-and-refill search that turns the masked decoder into an iterative local molecular optimizer. On CrossDocked2020, AdaFO increases Success Rate from 30.2\% to 70.8\%, the best reported under this protocol, while largely preserving drug-likeness and diversity. Finally, scaffold-preserving directional editing and CRBN/VHL case studies demonstrate its use in compound design workflows spanning local molecular editing, structure-based prioritization, and downstream simulation-based screening.</p></details> |  |
| **[PocketVE: Stable and Property-Guided Structure-Based Drug Design with Variance-Exploding Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.08101v3)** | 2026-09-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Protein-conditioned 3D molecule generation is a central challenge in structure-based drug design, requiring a balance between pocket compatibility, molecular properties, and physical geometry. We propose \textbf{PocketVE}, a protein-pocket-conditioned variance-exploding (VE) diffusion framework that couples stable coordinate denoising with inference-time property guidance. Specifically, PocketVE combines an EDM-style training and sampling setup for 3D denoising, classifier-free guidance for multi-property steering, and adaptive protein perturbation as a training-time pocket regularizer.On the CrossDocked2020 benchmark under GenBench3D, PocketVE improves Valid$_{3\text{D}}$ from 58.6 to 80.6 and reduces strain energy from 457.4 to 127.9 relative to its guided TAGMol architectural parent; relative to TargetDiff, it attains comparable Valid$_{3\text{D}}$ with lower strain energy (127.9 vs.\ 306.0), while retaining competitive docking and molecular-property scores under moderate guidance. Pocket-permutation and PoseCheck diagnostics further support pocket-specific spatial compatibility with reduced steric conflicts. Overall, the results suggest that geometric stability and inference-time property guidance should be considered as coupled design objectives.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted by Neurips 2026</p></details> |
| **[A Systematic Evaluation of Molecule Generation Models for De Novo Drug Design: From Benchmarks to Practical Insights](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.10099v1)** | 2026-09-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Molecule generation has emerged as a powerful computational tool for de novo drug design, enabling the exploration of chemical space beyond the limits of conventional virtual screening. The field has progressed rapidly, driven by advances in molecular representations, generative architectures, and target-aware modeling strategies. However, existing reviews typically address specific model families or application scenarios in isolation, rather than offering an integrated perspective on how these components collectively form a coherent generation workflow. In this review, we present a comprehensive evaluation of molecule generation models for de novo drug design, covering 82 methods across five deep generative frameworks, including recurrent neural network (RNN)- and Transformer-based models, variational autoencoders (VAEs), generative adversarial networks (GANs), flow-based models, and diffusion models. We first summarize widely used benchmarks and molecular representations, and then examine the methodological principles underlying both general and pocket-conditioned generation. A central contribution of this work is a systematic synthesis and comparative analysis of reported performance across commonly used benchmarks and evaluation metrics. We also summarize representative experimentally validated case studies. Looking ahead, we discuss future directions in standardized 3D data, interaction-aware generation, receptor flexibility, and multi-objective molecular design, with the aim of improving the reliability and experimental relevance of molecule generation. All collected benchmark resources, evaluation metrics, and model references are provided in a publicly accessible repository at https://github.com/JacklinGroup/molecule-generation-review.</p></details> | <details><summary>This ...</summary><p>This document is the unedited Author's version of a Submitted Manuscript subsequently accepted for publication in [Journal of Chemical Information and Modeling]. To access the final published article</p></details> |
| **[FuseDiff: Symmetry-Preserving Joint Diffusion for Dual-Target Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.05567v2)** | 2026-09-07 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Dual-target structure-based drug design aims to generate a single ligand together with two pocket-specific binding poses, each compatible with a corresponding target pocket, enabling polypharmacological therapies with improved efficacy and reduced resistance. FuseDiff features a message-passing backbone with Dual-target Local Context Fusion (DLCF), which fuses each ligand atom's local context from both pockets to enable expressive joint modeling while preserving the desired symmetries. Together with explicit bond generation, FuseDiff enforces topological consistency across the two poses under a shared graph while allowing target-specific geometric adaptation in each pocket. To support principled training and evaluation, we derive a dual-target training set and use an independent held-out test set for evaluation. Experiments on the benchmark and a real-world dual-target system show that FuseDiff achieves state-of-the-art docking performance and enables the first systematic assessment of dual-target pose quality prior to docking-based pose search.</p></details> | KDD 2026 |
| **[NEAT-POCKET: Pocket-Conditioned Autoregressive 3D Molecular Generation with a Neighborhood-Guided Set Transformer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05097v1)** | 2026-09-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>AI-driven de novo molecular design offers a promising route to accelerate early-stage drug discovery by generating novel ligands directly within target protein binding pockets. | **[SurfSpec: Enhancing Off-Target-Agnostic Specificity by Bounding Pocket-Ligand Geometric Mismatch](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.02963v1)** | 2026-09-02 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Lead optimization in structure-based drug design aims to improve target binding while avoiding unintended interactions with off-target pockets. | **[Risk-Sensitive Reward Composition for Conditional GFlowNets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.01929v1)** | 2026-09-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets) for structure-based drug design condition on one rigid protein structure. Populations carry simulation error, and biology dictates which conformations are deal-breakers, so a candidate that fails one is disqualified, not merely ranked lower. No standard rule captures this. We compose the reward from a conditional value-at-risk (CVaR), a worst-case score rule, and an ambiguity radius expressing distrust in the stated weights. Together, these define a family of targets, amortised by a single conditional GFlowNet. An exact-KL oracle, a copy trained on the true target, shows if a shortfall is the optimiser's or the architecture's. | **[MCCE: A Framework for Multi-LLM Collaborative Search in Discrete Spaces with Similarity-Filtered Preference Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.06270v2)** | 2026-08-27 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective discrete optimization problems, such as molecular design, pose significant challenges due to their vast and unstructured combinatorial spaces. Traditional evolutionary algorithms often get trapped in local optima, while expert knowledge can provide crucial guidance for accelerating convergence. Large language models (LLMs) offer powerful priors and reasoning ability, making them natural optimizers when expert knowledge matters. However, closed-source LLMs, though strong in exploration, cannot update their parameters and thus cannot internalize experience. | **[READ: A Retrieval-Alignment Diffusion Framework for Structure-based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.14488v2)** | 2026-08-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design (SBDD) models are central to modern pharmaceutical research, enabling the rational exploration of protein-ligand interactions at atomic resolution. Supplementary material is included as an ancillary file. Accepted for publication in IEEE Transactions on Computational Biology and Bioinformatics</p></details> |

Finally, unselected latent coordinates are resampled to generate diverse decodings of a single optimized solution. Preprint</p></details> |

| **[URSA: Chemistry-Aware Benchmark for Utilitarian Retrosynthesis Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.04688v1)** | 2026-07-06 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Synthesis planning aiming to find pathways of reactions for a target molecule is one of the most important and challenging tasks in drug discovery. This work introduces the Geometric OOD Diffusion Model (GODD), a novel diffusion-based framework that enables training on data-abundant molecular distributions while generalizing to data-scarce distributions under distributional structural shifts. Central to our approach is a designated equivariant asymmetric autoencoder to capture distributional structural priors. The asymmetric design allows the model to generalize to unseen structural variations by capturing distributional priors representing distinct distributions. The encoded structural-grained priors guide generation toward sparse regions without requiring explicit training on such data. Evaluated across standard benchmarks encompassing OOD structural shifts (e.g., scaffolds, rings), GODD achieves an improvement of 12.6% in success rate, defined based on molecular validity, uniqueness, and novelty. Furthermore, the framework demonstrates promising performance and generalization on canonical fragment-based drug design tasks, highlighting its utility in learning-based molecular discovery.</p></details> | <details><summary>24 pa...</summary><p>24 pages. Accepted by AAAI 2026</p></details> |
| **[FROGENT: An End-to-End Full-process Drug Design Multi-Agent System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.10760v2)** | 2026-03-01 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Drug discovery is a complex, multi-step pipeline that remains heavily dependent on manual, experience-driven operations; meanwhile, existing customized artificial intelligence tools are fragmented across web applications, desktop software, and code libraries, resulting in incompatible interfaces and inefficient, burdensome workflows. FROGENT is a collaborative multi-agent system comprising a central Orchestrate Agent for strategic workflow coordination and three distributed agents, Retrieve, Forge, and Gauge, that employ dynamic biochemical databases, extensible tool libraries, and task-specific computational models via the Model Context Protocol. This architecture enables end-to-end execution of complex drug discovery pipelines, covering target identification, small-molecule generation, peptide optimization, and retrosynthetic planning. Case studies further demonstrate its practicality and generalization across real-world small-molecule and peptide design scenarios. Overall, FROGENT not only achieves substantial gains in efficiency and accuracy, but also demonstrates the potential of LLM-based agentic systems to autonomously orchestrate drug development pipelines, reducing, or even replacing, reliance on manual, experience-driven human intervention.</p></details> | 37 pages, 20 figures |

DecompDPO introduces decomposition into the optimization objectives and obtains preference pairs at the molecule or decomposed substructure level based on each objective's decomposability. Additionally, DecompDPO introduces a physics-informed energy term to ensure reasonable molecular conformations in the optimization results. High Affinity and a 36.2% success rate for molecule generation, and 100% Med. High Affinity and a 52.1% success rate for molecular optimization. Code is available at https://github.com/laviaf/DecompDPO.</p></details> | Accepted by TMLR |

| **[Dynamics-inspired Structure Hallucination for Protein-protein Interaction Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.06214v1)** | 2026-01-08 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Protein-protein interaction (PPI) represents a central challenge within the biology field, and accurately predicting the consequences of mutations in this context is crucial for drug design and protein engineering. We present in this work the first comprehensive experimental analysis of this advantage of QGANS applied to SAT4 image generation, obtaining an exponential advantage in capacity scaling for a quantum generator in the hybrid latent style-based QGAN architecture. Once this tuning is performed and defining training optimality as when the training is stable and the FID score is low and stable as well, the optimal capacity (or number of trainable parameters) of the classical discriminator scales exponentially with respect to the capacity of the quantum generator, and the same is true for the capacity of the classical generator. | **[ReACT-Drug: Reaction-Template Guided Reinforcement Learning for de novo Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.20958v1)** | 2025-12-24 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>De novo drug design is a crucial component of modern drug development, yet navigating the vast chemical space to find synthetically accessible, high-affinity candidates remains a significant challenge. | **[Improvement of AMPs Identification with Generative Adversarial Network and Ensemble Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.01983v3)** | 2025-12-16 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Identification of antimicrobial peptides is an important and necessary issue in today's era. Suggested method is improved by combining the best coding method from different perspectives, In the following a deep neural network to balance the imbalanced combined datasets. The results of this research show that the proposed method have a significant improvement in the accuracy and efficiency of the prediction of antimicrobial peptides and are able to provide the best results compared to the existing methods. These development in the field of prediction and classification of antimicrobial peptides, basically in the fields of medicine and pharmaceutical industries, have high effectiveness and application.</p></details> | <details><summary>21 pa...</summary><p>21 pages, 3 figures, 4 tables</p></details> |
| **[DynaMate: An Autonomous Agent for Protein-Ligand Molecular Dynamics Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.10034v1)** | 2025-12-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Force field-based molecular dynamics (MD) simulations are indispensable for probing the structure, dynamics, and functions of biomolecular systems, including proteins and protein-ligand complexes. Despite their broad utility in drug discovery and protein engineering, the technical complexity of MD setup, encompassing parameterization, input preparation, and software configuration, remains a major barrier for widespread and efficient usage. Agentic LLMs have demonstrated their capacity to autonomously execute multi-step scientific processes, and to date, they have not successfully been used to automate protein-ligand MD workflows. Here, we present DynaMate, a modular multi-agent framework that autonomously designs and executes complete MD workflows for both protein and protein-ligand systems, and offers free energy binding affinity calculations with the MM/PB(GB)SA method. The framework integrates dynamic tool use, web search, PaperQA, and a self-correcting behavior. DynaMate comprises three specialized modules, interacting to plan the experiment, perform the simulation, and analyze the results. We evaluated its performance across twelve benchmark systems of varying complexity, assessing success rate, efficiency, and adaptability. DynaMate reliably performed full MD simulations, corrected runtime errors through iterative reasoning, and produced meaningful analyses of protein-ligand interactions. This automated framework paves the way toward standardized, scalable, and time-efficient molecular modeling pipelines for future biomolecular and drug design applications.</p></details> |  |
| **[OMTRA: A Multi-Task Generative Model for Structure-Based Drug Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.05080v1)** | 2025-12-04 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Structure-based drug design (SBDD) focuses on designing small-molecule ligands that bind to specific protein pockets. Computational methods are integral in modern SBDD workflows and often make use of virtual screening methods via docking or pharmacophore search. Modern generative modeling approaches have focused on improving novel ligand discovery by enabling de novo design. In this work, we recognize that these tasks share a common structure and can therefore be represented as different instantiations of a consistent generative modeling framework. We propose a unified approach in OMTRA, a multi-modal flow matching model that flexibly performs many tasks relevant to SBDD, including some with no analogue in conventional workflows. Additionally, we curate a dataset of 500M 3D molecular conformers, complementing protein-ligand data and expanding the chemical diversity available for training. OMTRA obtains state of the art performance on pocket-conditioned de novo design and docking; however, the effects of large-scale pretraining and multi-task training are modest. All code, trained models, and dataset for reproducing this work are available at https://github.com/gnina/OMTRA</p></details> | <details><summary>Prese...</summary><p>Presented at the Machine Learning for Structural Biology Workshop, 2025</p></details> |

## multi-objective optimization
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| **[Progressive Lower Bound Evaluation for Urban Road Renovation Scheduling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15554v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Urban infrastructure degrades over time, necessitating periodic renovation to maintain functionality and safety. When renovation is delayed beyond the infrastructure's remaining lifespan, costly emergency interventions become necessary to prevent failure. Decision makers must therefore balance expected emergency intervention costs against traffic congestion impacts. We formalize this trade-off as a road network maintenance scheduling problem with uncertain deadlines, which presents optimization challenges including computationally expensive evaluation and an exponentially growing solution space. We formulate the problem as a bi-level multi-objective optimization problem that explicitly accounts for uncertain infrastructure lifespans through probabilistic failure models. We develop a progressive lower bound evaluation method: candidate schedules receive quick, optimistic estimates of their traffic impact from machine learning based surrogate models, and these estimates are refined by exact traffic simulation only for promising candidates, so that full evaluation is reserved almost exclusively for schedules that can advance the Pareto front. Embedded in a multi-objective genetic algorithm, this evaluation strategy improves solution quality by enabling many more iterations within a fixed computational budget. We demonstrate the method's effectiveness and scalability on substantially larger problem instances than previously addressed in the literature: 76 and 80 projects on the Sioux Falls and Anaheim networks, respectively. Under an identical 24-hour computational budget across 30 random seeds, the method completes up to 87 times as many optimization iterations as standard NSGA-II and achieves statistically significant improvements across multiple performance metrics over both standard NSGA-II and a surrogate-assisted baseline.</p></details> | <details><summary>31 pa...</summary><p>31 pages, 12 figures. Accepted manuscript of the article published in Computer-Aided Civil and Infrastructure Engineering, https://doi.org/10.1016/j.cacaie.2026.100174</p></details> |
| **[In-Context Preference-Conditioned Pareto Set Learning from Limited Offline Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21736v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Multi-objective optimization involves diverse preferences over conflicting objectives. Rather than returning a finite set of trade-off solutions, preference-conditioned Pareto set learning seeks to map a specified preference directly to a corresponding Pareto optimal solution. Yet in many real-world scenarios, optimization must proceed offline without further objective evaluations, leaving only a limited static dataset that sparsely covers the decision and objective spaces. Existing methods either optimize separately for each preference or train a problem-specific Pareto solution model, requiring repeated search across preferences or repeated training across problems. We introduce in-context preference-conditioned Pareto set learning (ICPSL), a framework that amortizes both preference-wise solution search and problem-specific model construction. ICPSL pretrains a foundation-style hypernetwork on a large and diverse set of synthetic prior data. Given the offline observations of an unseen problem, the pretrained hypernetwork learns to generate a problem-specific, preference-conditioned Pareto solution model in a single forward pass. The generated model can then be reused to predict solutions for arbitrary preferences without problem-specific training or preference-wise search. Experiments on synthetic and real-world problems show that ICPSL produces high-quality Pareto solutions while providing substantial speedups in problem-specific solution-model construction.</p></details> |  |
| **[LOADBench: A Large Open Analog Dataset and Benchmark for Machine Learning in IC Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21591v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Machine learning (ML) for analog IC design is constrained by limited open data and the absence of standardized evaluation: results reported in different papers are typically obtained on bespoke, often closed, datasets and with ad-hoc metrics, leaving comparisons across methods unreliable. We address both gaps with a large opensource dataset and a suite of standardized benchmarks. The dataset contains more than 100 operational amplifier (opamp) topologies (1-, 2-, and 3-stage) in the SkyWater130 PDK, each shipped with structural and functional annotations, topology-specific sizing constraints (symmetry and sizing rules), an Ngspice testbench, and a multi-objective NSGA-II sizing run producing around 103 Pareto-optimal and 106 intermediate sizing-performance pairs per topology. On top of this data we define six benchmark tasks with precise protocols: inverse sizing, topology selection, sub-circuit classification, structural circuit generation, Pareto-membership prediction, and multi-objective optimization. Thus, the data presented here constitutes the most comprehensive dataset for ML development in over four decades of analog IC design automation. The dataset, ground-truth annotations, and reference implementations of the metric-defining routines are released under an open license, enabling reproducible and directly comparable evaluation across heterogeneous analog design automation methods.</p></details> | <details><summary>9 pag...</summary><p>9 pages, 6 figures, Conference: MLCAD '26: 8th ACM/IEEE International Symposium on Machine Learning for CAD, September 7 to 9, 2026, Jeju, South Korea</p></details> |
| **[First Mathematical Runtime Analyses of Evolutionary Multitask Multi-Objective Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21311v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To facilitate the solving of complex problems, evolutionary multitask optimization is regarded as a promising technique due to its capacity for cross-task knowledge transfer. We analyze how the representative multiobjective multifactorial evolutionary algorithm (MO-MFEA) covers the Pareto fronts of two bi-objective problems simultaneously. | **[Enabling Validation for Robust Few-Shot Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2506.04713v4)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>A key challenge in Few-Shot Recognition (FSR) is the scarcity of labeled task-specific data for training. However, they largely overlook an equally fundamental challenge: the absence of validation data for model selection and hyperparameter tuning. As a result, the adapted VLMs overfit the few-shot training data and generalize poorly to OOD test data. We show that retrieved data provide a natural source of validation, but their direct use creates a fundamental paradox. Since the retrieved samples are inherently OOD w.r.t the few-shot ID training data, adapting a VLM to the ID data constantly degrades its performance on the retrieved data. Consequently, conventional validation relying on the accuracy of retrieved data favors no adaptation and prevents improved generalization. To resolve this dilemma, we formulate validation as a multi-objective optimization (MOO) problem between performance gains on the few-shot ID data and the performance preservation on the retrieved data. We introduce a simple yet effective validation strategy by choosing an operating point on the Pareto front of the MOO problem. | **[SURF: Steering the Scalarization Weight to Uniformly Traverse the Pareto Front](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20619v2)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Scalarization is widely used in multi-objective optimization owing to its simplicity and scalability. | **[Scheduling in Cloud,Fog, and Edge Computing: A Comparative Analysis and Decision Framework for Technique Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.13830v1)** | 2026-10-09 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>The increasing heterogeneity and dynamism of cloud, fog, and edge computing make scheduling a challenging problem involving diverse resource capabilities, workload characteristics, latency and energy constraints, and competing optimization objectives. This review presents a comparative analysis of task, job, and workflow scheduling across the cloud, fog, and edge paradigms, covering heuristic, metaheuristic, learning-based, and hybrid techniques. The analysis highlights trade-offs among computational efficiency, optimization capability, adaptability, and implementation complexity across technique families. Heuristic methods offer low computational overhead for relatively stable scheduling conditions, whereas metaheuristic methods are suited to complex and multi-objective optimization problems.
