While its latent consistency flow objective drives stable and efficient few-step inference, a Latent Consistency Perceptual Loss (LCPL) imposes semantic constraints directly on the guiding velocity field, steering the dynamics toward visually sharp data manifolds. Furthermore, recognizing the inherent conflict between structural and perceptual consistencies, we integrate a conflict-free gradient projection strategy to stabilize the multi-objective optimization landscape. Combined with lightweight, convolution-only backbone, PCFlow achieves competitive performance across diverse restoration tasks at a fraction of traditional computational costs.</p></details> | <details><summary>Accep...</summary><p>Accepted to ECCV 2026. Code is available at https://github.com/aiimaginglab/PCFlow</p></details> |
| **[Don't Retrain, Just Reuse: Recovering Dual-Target Molecules from Single-Target Diffusion Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25681v2)** | 2026-08-10 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Designing a single molecule that modulates two targets is a promising strategy for polypharmacology, but it remains substantially harder than standard single-target generation because one candidate must satisfy two binding requirements while preserving drug-likeness and synthesizability. ## DNA
| **Title** | **Date** | **Abstract** | **Comment** |

| **[JEPA-DNA: Grounding Genomic Foundation Models through Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.17162v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Genomic Foundation Models (GFMs) typically rely on Masked Language Modeling (MLM) or Next-Token Prediction (NTP) to learn the "Laws of Nature". Through extensive ablation studies, we further characterize the synergistic interplay between generative and latent objectives. Our code is provided in the supplementary material.</p></details> |  |

NTv3 improves 46 of 57 datasets (mean $Δ$MCC = +0.072), while Caduceus provides smaller gains and the other models do not improve mean MCC. We separately decompose embeddings into a component linearly predictable from 6-mer frequencies and a ridge residual, finding that linear recoverability does not reliably indicate downstream utility. Exact 6-mer-preserving shuffles further reveal task-relevant sequence-order sensitivity, most pronounced for NTv3, although order sensitivity alone does not guarantee predictive improvements. | **[Evaluation Awareness Beyond English: Do LLMs Act Differently When Tested in Indic Languages?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22333v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) can often tell when they are being tested. This ability, known as evaluation awareness, can lead models to act more cautiously during safety evaluations than in real use. Existing work has studied evaluation awareness almost entirely in English, although models serve users in many languages. Indic languages are spoken by over a billion people, yet evaluation awareness remains almost entirely unstudied in them. We test four instruction-tuned models from three families in five Indic languages alongside English. A set of cues varies whether the model is told that it is monitored and whether the monitoring is a safety test. Models refuse harmful requests more often when they are told that they are being tested. This increase appears in all four models and all six languages. In Indic languages, refusal of harmful requests rises by 14.4 percentage points on average. The test cue also brings Indic refusal of harmful requests level with English, but raises refusal of benign Indic requests from 6.9% to 11.3%. We release all 47,880 judged responses to support further work. These results suggest that safety evaluations in Indic languages may not reflect how models treat their everyday users.</p></details> |  |
| **[Learning to Steer LLM Agents and Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.22316v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM)-based agents and multi-agent systems increasingly tackle complex tasks through long-horizon tool use and inter-agent collaboration. However, these systems are prone to deviating from the overall goal as execution unfolds, which wastes execution resources and leads to undesired outcomes, a failure termed goal drift. In this work, we propose a steering agent that supervises execution on the fly and steers the system back on track when drift occurs. The steering agent observes the execution context, deciding when to intervene with a low-cost fast path and how to intervene with a high-cost slow path. To unlock its steering intelligence, the ability to assess goal drift and generate effective interventions, we jointly train the two paths with reinforcement learning, combining a pairwise ranking objective with group-relative policy optimization (GRPO). Experiments on information-seeking search tasks show that the steering agent provides effective supervision for both single-agent and multi-agent systems, improving their performance and efficiency. The learned steering intelligence generalizes to out-of-domain tasks, transfers to unseen executors, and supports controllable intervention frequency without retraining.</p></details> | 21 pages, 6 figures |

Given an initial grid, explicit rules, and an event history expressed as text, a model predicts the complete final grid. Reference execution makes every predicted value checkable, supporting comparisons across spatial operations, history lengths, and workloads. Evaluating six LLMs shows that complete-state accuracy falls sharply as histories grow, with substantial divergence among models that perform similarly on single events. Models with similar exact-match accuracy can nevertheless differ substantially in the number of incorrect cells on shared instances. Even over long histories, some models recover many correct changes beyond what copying the initial state would yield. Grid-Bench makes both complete recovery and these differences in returned states directly measurable across a range of spatial conditions.</p></details> | <details><summary>39 pa...</summary><p>39 pages, 15 figures, 15 tables, including appendices. | **[When is a better LLM worth paying for? Stochastic Optimization with Switching Mixed-Fidelity Oracles](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21139v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used upstream of decision-making to clean data, detect label errors, and reconstruct missing values. Yet LLM-assisted data curation creates a new operational tradeoff: higher-fidelity models and richer prompts can be substantially more expensive, while the value of this additional fidelity depends on the downstream optimization accuracy. This raises the central question of this work: when should one pay for a better LLM, given a specific downstream task at hand? We study this question through stochastic optimization with mixed-fidelity first-order oracles, which can have systematic bias and state-dependent variance. We develop two cost-aware algorithmic frameworks that allow adaptive switching between stochastic oracles. With known bias, the first meta-algorithm applies a restarted, cost-aware policy that adaptively selects oracle fidelity across optimization stages. Its cumulative cost is no larger than that of the best fixed oracle in hindsight and can be substantially smaller. With unknown bias, we develop a budgeted certification scheme that uses occasional trusted-gradient evaluations to certify the optimization progress and eliminate unsuccessful oracle sources, and show an explicit tradeoff between certification cost and delayed detection of biased sources. We provide concrete realizations of these meta-algorithms by applying biased stochastic gradient extrapolation (SGE) and recursive regularization under state-dependent noise as subroutines. Synthetic and LLM-completion experiments illustrate the qualitative switching behavior predicted by the theory: the adaptive policy skips several expensive LLM oracles and substantially reduces cumulative cost relative to fixed high-fidelity alternatives while achieving comparable accuracy.</p></details> |  |
| **[Mind the Delay: Latency-aware Query Routing for Heterogeneous LLM pools](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18253v2)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language model (LLM) query routers typically assign each query to a suitable model that balances response accuracy and monetary cost. However, decoupling accuracy-cost and latency considerations can lead to suboptimal routing decisions. We introduce the Batch Evolution Aware (BEA) estimator to provide a fine-grained estimate of the latency, that is, the time-to-first-token (TTFT) experienced by an arriving query. Unlike existing load balancers that rely on coarse signals such as queue lengths, per-model processing rates, or historical average latencies, BEA replays the serving framework's token-batch formation using a snapshot of an instance's current workload, stopping when the incoming query's first token is generated. Taking into account the compute and memory requirements of the prefill and decode phases of language queries as well as the contention between multiple queries that are served in the same batch, BEA predicts TTFT within an error of approximately 5%. Each prediction takes about 0.1 ms, which is fast enough to run on the critical routing path. We also design an analytical framework for a model instance under a limited processor-sharing scheme that calculates expected TTFT in closed form and captures the qualitative trend of latencies as the load increases. The TTFT estimates produced by BEA are then used in our latency-aware router, BEAR, which simultaneously balances accuracy, cost, and latency to select the best model instance from a heterogeneous pool of LLMs. Against seven baselines, BEAR improves the utility of responses delivered within their targets by 63% on average over existing latency-aware routers, and by 30.5% over the best baseline at each arrival rate.</p></details> |  |

A PAC-style bound shows that tampers with zero output shift are undetectable from outputs at any sample size; a manifest-hash identity channel detects them with zero samples. | **[LLMON: An LLM-native Markup Language to Leverage Structure and Semantics at the LLM Interface](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.22519v4)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Textual Large Language Models (LLMs) provide a simple and familiar interface: a string of text is used for both input and output. This work addresses this shortcoming by introducing an LLM-native mark-up language, LLMON (LLM Object Notation, pronounced "Lemon"), that enables the structure and semantic metadata of the text to be communicated in a natural way to an LLM. We discuss the general design requirements of an LLM-native markup language, introduce the LLMON markup language and show how it meets these design requirements, describe how the information contained in a LLMON artifact can benefit model training and inference implementation, and provide some preliminary empirical evidence of its value for both of these use cases. | **[Planning Commitment of LLM under Partial Observability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21108v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>LLM-based planning agents commit to plans of different lengths before interacting with a partially observable environment. We investigate aggressive and conservative LLM planners and formalizers on two partially observable domains, where priors are provided as either explicit statistics or common-sense knowledge. Across three LLMs, both strategies reach similar accuracy. Conservative methods achieve fewer failed attempts (safety) at the cost of more API calls (efficiency). Aggressive methods fail more often but replan efficiently only when priors are informative. Interestingly, analysis shows that LLMs treat the provided priors nearly as facts, which risks failure when priors are unreliable. We also explore hybrid strategies with tunable hyperparameters that adjust the balance between safety and efficiency.</p></details> |  |

| **[Bellman-Consistent Trajectory RL for LLM Arithmetic Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21060v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>To address sparse terminal rewards in LLM arithmetic reasoning, we propose a Bellman-consistent trajectory RL method. | **[INCONVENIENT: A Benchmark to Assess LLM Generalization Capabilities from Convenience Samples](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21038v1)** | 2026-10-15 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Machine learning models for tabular prediction typically assume that training data are representative of the population on which they will be deployed. In practice, however, data are often collected based on convenience, availability, or cost, resulting in systematic sampling biases that can distort feature--target relationships and undermine real-world generalization. Existing tabular distribution-shift benchmarks primarily construct out-of-distribution (OOD) settings by partitioning observations across naturally occurring domains. While such partitions may induce changes in P(Y | X), the resulting shifts are neither explicitly controlled nor isolated, and the evaluation distributions themselves are often not representative of the broader target population. We introduce INCONVENIENT, a benchmark designed to study robustness to convenience-sample bias. Rather than relying on naturally occurring domain partitions, INCONVENIENT directly perturbs feature--target relationships in the training distribution while preserving the original population distribution as the deployment environment. This setting more closely reflects real-world scenarios in which models are developed from unrepresentative samples and subsequently deployed on a broader population. The distinction is particularly relevant for large language models (LLMs), whose pretraining may encode population-level knowledge that extends beyond the relationships observed in a biased training dataset. Unlike existing distribution-shift benchmarks, INCONVENIENT provides explicit control over both the direction and severity of relationship shifts, enabling a systematic assessment of robustness to convenience sampling. The benchmark comprises 200 tabular classification and regression datasets spanning diverse application domains and generates around 15,500 shifted training distributions which lead to substantial downstream degradation.</p></details> |  |

At the cluster level, ADS organizes samples according to semantic patterns and adapts the inter-cluster sampling distribution to prioritize clusters within the policy's current reach. At the sample level, ADS tracks prompts near the policy's capability boundary within each cluster to obtain informative relative advantages. | **[Agent Gym: A Framework for Continuous Evaluation and Evolution of LLM Agents Through Human-in-the-Loop Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15591v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Model (LLM) agents deployed in production environments face a fundamental tension: the agent's behavior is frozen at deployment time, while the business rules and edge cases it must handle continue to evolve. We introduce Agent Gym, a modular, domain-agnostic framework that wraps any existing LLM-based agent in a continuous evaluation-and-evolution loop. The framework provides six composable capabilities --- Act, Evaluate, Investigate, Correct, Learn, and Observe --- organized across three architectural zones: a constitution layer that codifies domain knowledge in configuration artifacts, a runtime inference pipeline that chains acting, investigation, and adaptive correction, and a learning loop that enables subject matter experts to discover and validate new correction rules through natural language interaction. Paired skill replacements against shared background rules are scored on the same feedback cases; least squares converts the score differences into relative skill values, and the top-ranked rules are retained, favoring incumbents on ties. Across the recorded training configurations, which use different optimization budgets, it consumes 55.6% fewer training tokens than SkillOpt. Gains concentrate in particular calculations alongside regressions on others, and paired accuracy intervals include zero. CausalEvo turns task feedback into controlled decisions about skill retention and replacement. Project repository: https://github.com/txbrook/CausalEvo</p></details> | <details><summary>GenAI...</summary><p>GenAI for Health Workshop @ NeurIPS 2026, Sydney</p></details> |

Presents T-GADE as a general diversity-aware framework for LLM-based evolutionary computation with an EoH-aligned implementation example, reports a preregistered seed-paired comparison in which the host-equipped configuration reaches low excess with fewer total LLM calls than EoH, and treats the selection temperature as an explicit diversity control</p></details> |

On MultiHopRAG model diversity (k=2), the repetition gap Rep=-8.33 pp [-13.25, -3.75] quantifies the difference between gains measured against Duplicate and Single-slot. On a matched FRAMES stronger-panel extension (n=200, k=3, aggregator output limit 4096), paired Delta Rep=+9.67 pp [+6.00, +13.50]; within panels, Rep changes sign (original -6.33; stronger +3.33). | **[Neither True nor False: Do LLMs Represent Truth-Value Gaps in Plural Predication?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14438v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly being used to summarize and verify claims in critical sources that inform consequential decisions. Such claims are full of plural definites. If three of five patients improved after taking drug A, the claim the patients improved after drug A is not true, since two did not, yet its negation, the patients didn't improve after drug A, is not true either, since three did. In formal semantics, this truth-value gap is known as the Homogeneity Gap: both the plural definite and its negation are neither true nor false when the property holds for only a fraction of the plural referent. A model that cannot represent this gap turns mixed evidence into a clean verdict, an error that can be costly in high-stakes use. We introduce HomogeneityNLI, a diagnostic dataset of 1,000 minimal pair items crossing determiner, polarity, and group composition across ten logical conditions. Evaluating seven open-weight models, we uncover a pervasive Bivalence Bias: models assign plural definites their homogeneous truth conditions. In the zero-shot setting, no model recognizes the gap for both polarities in more than 15% of scenarios, and negated definites are judged False in 84% of cases. Explicit linguistic rules, chain-of-thought, and enumeration prompts produce unstable, model-specific effects that often entrench the bias. We further demonstrate positive transfer from Twenty Questions training to Medi-Q, a real-world clinical dialogue benchmark. These results suggest that information gathering can be learned as a generalizable reasoning skill in LLMs.</p></details> |  |
| **[Towards Better Aligned LLM Judges for Radiology Report Evaluation across Modalities and Anatomies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.03376v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current work on radiology report evaluation has focused primarily on LLM-based metrics for chest X-rays (CXR), leaving open whether such approaches remain robust across other modalities and anatomies, and which model and prompt configurations are best suited to serve as LLM judges. We present a comprehensive evaluation of LLM judges for radiology report evaluation across diverse model families, prompting strategies, and adaptation methods, leveraging two expert-annotated datasets that span multiple imaging modalities and anatomical regions. Specifically, we compare four existing metrics (RadFact, GREEN, FineRadScore, and CRIMSON) with our proposed metric, VERT, across open- and closed-source models of varying sizes and reasoning capabilities. Among existing metrics, GREEN proves the most robust, outperforming more recent CXR-specific approaches. VERT further improves Kendall's correlation with radiologist judgment by up to 26% over GREEN, while ensembling and lightweight fine-tuning of Qwen3-30B yield gains of up to 9.9% and 25%, respectively, of which the latter also has a latency reduction of 37.2x. A systematic error analysis further shows that, while these metrics correlate with expert ratings overall, they tend to miscount errors such as omissions. Overall, our results show that carefully selected general-purpose LLM judges provide strong radiology report evaluation across modalities, with lightweight adaptation offering complementary gains when annotated data is available.</p></details> |  |
| **[Optimal Hydra: Model Heterogeneous Federated Learning for LLMs using Data-Informed Hydra-Nets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20753v1)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Large language models (LLMs) trained on vast amounts of data have demonstrated impressive performance over a range of tasks, but future performance gains will require increasing amounts of data and compute. Federated Learning (FL) facilitates training on decentralized private data, but client resource limitations can make training impractical, particularly as model sizes continue to expand. These resource constraints make the assumption of a shared server-client architecture impractical. Model heterogeneous FL offers a promising alternative by adapting model size to client capabilities; however, previous work is limited to the pre-training setting, and conclusions on sub-network selection do not necessarily hold for fine-tuning. Furthermore, prior approaches create client sub-networks without considering their unique data distributions, thereby overlooking the opportunity to tailor sub-network architecture to local data. Motivated by evidence that attention heads exhibit task dependency within layer defined feature spaces, we propose Optimal Hydra, a transformer based heterogeneous FL framework for fine-tuning that combines global layer selection with client specific attention head selection, aligned with local data distributions. Optimal Hydra makes explicit use of client data to produce client-specific sub-networks we call Hydra-nets. To our knowledge, this is the first model heterogeneous FL approach tailored to LLM fine-tuning. Experiments on three datasets show significant improvements over baseline methods while also reducing local resource demands.</p></details> | <details><summary>curre...</summary><p>currently under submission</p></details> |
| **[Evolutionary Multi-Task Optimization for LLM-Guided Program Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22613v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Recent LLM-guided evolutionary search methods have shown that iterative program mutation can discover strong algorithms, but they typically optimize each task independently, even when related tasks share reusable structure. We study evolutionary multitask optimization (EMO) for LLM-guided program discovery and propose EMO-STA (Shared-Then-Adapt), a two-stage framework that first evolves a shared archive of executable programs across a task family and then adapts selected shared candidates to each target task. Within EMO-STA, we explore multiple adaptation strategies, including warm-starting from the shared archive, adapting the best average shared program, and adapting the shared program that performs best on each target task. Across eight task families spanning continuous optimization, geometric construction, modeling, and algorithmic optimization, EMO-STA improves over single-task evolution under matched evolutionary-iteration budgets in most settings, with STA Best-Local providing the strongest in-distribution adaptation and STA Best-Shared yielding robust transfer to unseen tasks. Iteration-allocation experiments show that devoting a substantial fraction of the family-level iteration budget to shared evolution is effective, with roughly balanced shared and aggregate adaptation budgets often yielding the best performance. ARC and time-series feature-engineering case studies further suggest that shared evolution can mitigate overfitting in low-evidence settings by favoring programs that perform well across related tasks rather than exploiting brittle task-specific artifacts.</p></details> |  |
| **[Surfacing Neural Activations Over Multi-Turn Conversation Improves User Calibration to LLM Behavioral Drift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15455v2)** | 2026-10-14 | <details><summary>Show</summary><p>Current human-AI interaction (HAI) only surfaces model outputs, hiding how LLM behavior can shift unpredictably toward unsafe behaviors in multi-turn conversations, potentially undermining users' goals or wellbeing. To address this harmful opacity in multi-turn HAI, we introduce an interface that surfaces an LLM's internal activations to help users anticipate and evaluate model behavior. We construct behavioral scores from these activations for six HAI-relevant traits using mechanistic interpretability methods, then visualize them via a dynamic sunburst, drift panel, and attentional cues. In a counterbalanced, randomized controlled study (N=246), both static and dynamic transparency interfaces improved participants' anticipation and evaluation performance (d=-0.36) while helping temper overconfidence in their predictive abilities. Furthermore, the multi-turn interface significantly outperformed the static visualization (d=-0.32) while remaining similarly usable. Our findings suggest that continuously surfacing internal model representations to everyday users is a meaningful and tractable step toward continuously transparent and informed HAI.</p></details> |  |
